# TO MELCHIOR, BISHOP OF BASEL,
## Most Illustrious Prince,

->Basilius Johannes Herold sends greetings.<- <note>The original Latin "S." stands for "Salutem," a standard greeting meaning "wishes health" or "sends greetings."</note>

Within the circular counter of the letter, a figure is depicted kneeling in a state of prayer or contemplation, set against a background of foliage and distant landscape. The corners of the square block containing the letter are filled with ornamental foliage.</image-desc>

SINCE indeed, Melchior, most illustrious Prince <note>As Bishop of Basel, Melchior held the rank of Prince-Bishop, a title signifying both religious and secular authority within the Holy Roman Empire.</note>, that great—nay, I say, that greatest God of ours—could bestow nothing at all upon man (whom He perfected after His own likeness and image) greater than the soul, it is established by that very reasoning that the soul is the finest of all gifts. For the bestower and preserver of the human race, the giver of so great a good, demands this one thing from us; and once it is offered to Him, it is wondrous with what kindness and mercy He receives it. Since <note>original: "Qua"; this is a catchword, a fragment of the first word of the following page intended to help the bookbinder.</note>

# PREFACE

because, with its generosity and authority, that most noble nature and condition of yours, **MELCHIOR**, Prince, displays a certain image of that Divinity who loves and rules the universe, I do not doubt at all that if I offer my devotion—which I have long owed to you—through this small sign and testimony, you will receive it with your innate kindness and benevolence. I therefore dedicate to you, Prince **MELCHIOR**, this **COMPENDIUM OF ALCHEMY** by the most distinguished **JOHN GARLAND** <note>Johannes de Garlandia (c. 1190 – c. 1270). While historically known as a grammarian, several influential alchemical treatises were attributed to him in the Renaissance.</note>, a truly golden little book, crafted with a clear explanation of the art it teaches. All learned men attest that no art is older than this one, and its very results demonstrate that none is more divine:

# PREFACE

Because your most noble nature and standing, Prince Melchior <note>Melchior von Lichtenfels, who served as the Prince-Bishop of Basel from 1554 to 1575</note>, with its generosity and authority, reflects a certain image of that Divinity who loves and rules the universe, I have no doubt that you will receive—with your innate kindness and benevolence—my devotion, which I have long owed to you, if I offer it through this small sign and testimony.

I therefore dedicate to you, Prince Melchior, this **Compendium of Alchemy** by the most distinguished John Garland <note>John of Garland (c. 1190–1270), a renowned scholar; while primarily known for grammar, several alchemical texts were attributed to him during the Renaissance</note>, a truly golden booklet <note>original: "libellum certè aureum"; a common play on words in alchemical texts, suggesting the book is as valuable as the gold the art seeks to create</note>, crafted with a clear explanation of the art it teaches. Moreover, all learned men testify that no art is older than this, and its results themselves demonstrate that none is more divine:

This is done through a certain sweet contemplation of the wonders of God, which—throughout the practical work <note>original: "operatione"; referring to the labor in the laboratory</note> that this art requires—reveals itself everywhere with great joy. # PREFACE

...and at what times each matter should be handled, it cannot be hidden from you. "Aha!" they will say, "does a client <term>cliens: a dependent or subordinate seeking the protection and favor of a powerful person</term> thrust such things—so foreign to his own studies, even more foreign to the dignity of a Prince, and most foreign of all to the consultations and deliberations required by these times of ours—upon a Patron <term>Patronus: a protector or benefactor of high social standing</term> to whom he confesses he owes everything? A fine debt indeed! <note>original: "bonum nomen"; literally "a good name" or "a good debt." In a financial context, this refers to a debt that is likely to be repaid, here used sarcastically by the author's imagined critics to mock his offering.</note>" 

I am certainly less troubled by these slanders because I am more convinced that I have, for the most part, satisfied both my private and public debt. For it serves one’s integrity <note>original: "Fidei"; meaning trust, faith, or credit</note> to remain mindful of a debt in the presence of the creditor, even while the actual payment is hindered by some difficulty. 

Therefore, I preferred to take out a new loan <term>versura: a financial maneuver where one borrows money to pay off an existing debt, effectively rolling over the obligation</term> and pay the standard interest rate <term>centesima: the "hundredth" part, referring to a standard monthly interest rate of one percent common in Roman law</term>, rather than be crushed by the most unfair compound interest <term>anatocismus: the practice of charging interest upon interest, which could lead to ruinous debt</term>, until those thirty books regarding the Origin...

I shall consider myself to have satisfied, in some way, **ANNI VITOSCHEYB**, the Decurion <term>Decurioni: a member of a city council or administrative official</term>, **GEORGE von AMPRINGEN**, and the other members of your sacred college <note>referring to the Cathedral Chapter of Basel, the body of advisors to the Prince-Bishop</note>, who strongly urge that I satisfy you—though you are one who can in no way be fully satisfied by me.

# PREFACE

That physician, **JOHANN HUBER**, a citizen and my greatest friend, will easily judge this. Those who thirst for true and absolute skill in such a great art will also sense its value; as for the unskilled, I do not concern myself with them. For I understand that such people argue too dryly and thinly about that supreme wisdom of yours—that wisdom which neither the weight of your office, nor the bitterness of the disagreements and altercations of our time <note>a likely reference to the religious and political turmoil of the Reformation era in the mid-16th century</note>, could ever move or turn aside from your steadfast purpose and plan to aid the Republic <term>Reipublicae: here referring to the state or the public welfare of the Bishopric</term>.

Nothing has deterred you from undertaking those institutions and investigating and bringing forth the best methods for the tranquility of the fatherland. By these means, you have earned the favor of your ancestors, cultivated the friendship of your peers, and confirmed the goodwill of your neighbors and subjects...

...you might excite the love [of your subjects], and finally show yourself to all as one whom they found to have combined the arts and concerns of a good man and an excellent prince with the timely study of literature <note>original: "bonarum literarum"—the humanities or classical learning</note>. They affirm that **HERMES TRISMEGISTUS**, the Egyptian philosopher, was unwilling to reveal the secrets of the **CHEMICAL ART** <term>artis Chymiæ: alchemy or early chemistry</term>—which he had received from his ancestors—to anyone during his entire life. But as he was dying, they say he wrote down a few elements of this science and ordered that they be placed in his tomb along with his body. Moved by a certain premonition of the mind, he sensed that this art, buried with himself, would after a long time be discovered by certain pious and learned...

# PREFACE

...to be restored by learned men. Therefore, these words—whether truly handed down by him <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus</note>, or invented by someone else to lend authority to this art—our friend **GARLAND** <note>likely John Garland, a 13th-century scholar whose name was frequently associated with alchemical compendiums</note> set out to interpret for the initiated in a very beautiful and distinct order. Indeed, he accomplished this with the clearest and simplest of words. But because he had striven for brevity, **ARNOLD OF VILLANOVA** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), one of the most famous medieval physicians and alchemists</note> added a more extensive explanation so that the work might be more lucid and transparent, and so that the author's meaning could be fully grasped from it. Truly, since every art has its own specific and particularly appropriate vocabulary <term>uocabula: technical terminology used to define specific concepts or tools</term> by which its objects and instruments are designated, our author here converted all the **FOREIGN TERMS** <note>original: "barbaras appellationes"—literally "barbarous names," usually referring to the Arabic technical terms that dominated early alchemical literature</note> into the Latin language,

# PREFACE

which he thought were suitable for this purpose, so that those who might devote themselves to investigating this science would not be delayed by unfamiliar terminology <term>nomenclatione: the system of names or terms used in a particular field of study</term>. He then wrote regarding the **tincturing of hard materials** <term>infectio materiarum: the alchemical process of coloring or "staining" metals, often to give base metals the appearance of gold or silver</term>, which are excavated from the veins of the earth. Whatever the quality of that writing may be, we have published it as well, in order to expose the deception of certain scoundrels who, through such operations, counterfeit the **coinage** <note>original: "monetam"</note> of gold, silver, and also copper of every kind. 

For to speak frankly, **LEONHARD PROEBSTEL** of Füssen <note>Leonhard Proebstel (d. 1555) was a mathematician and friend of the author, likely associated with the intellectual circles of Augsburg</note>—a man miraculously learned in the knowledge of natural things and **mathematics** <term>Mathesi: in the 16th century, this term often encompassed the broader "mathematical sciences," including astronomy, mechanics, and geometry</term>, and my very dear friend—had seriously warned me of this trickery. Therefore, I hope that this announcement of mine, joined with this friendly warning, may be all the more approved by

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface addressed to Prince Melchior. It continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the importance of identifying counterfeit alchemical practices and the inclusion of a supplementary text on mineral salts.</meta>

# PREFACE

I trust everyone will understand how much less we should neglect the fraud of these most greedy scoundrels <term>grassatorum: literally "assailants" or "highwaymen," used here to describe deceptive counterfeiters who "attack" the economy</term>. Indeed, as for that description in which various **salts** and **alums** <term>alumina: a group of mineral salts used in metallurgy, dyeing, and alchemy</term> are contained—although in the manuscript we used it is attributed to our author—I would more willingly credit it to some learned Spaniard. Regardless of its origin, however, it can be of great use to the practitioners of this art. 

But lest the **preface** <note>original: "prooemium"</note> to the work become too long, I will "withdraw my hand from the tablet" <note>a Latin idiom (manum de tabula) meaning to finish a task or stop working on a painting to avoid over-refining it</note>, as the saying goes, if I only add this one thing: that I, **Prince MELCHIOR**, uniquely strive so that by my own merit and by the judgment of your Greatness, I might prove worthy of being most highly and perpetually commended to you. 

Farewell, most distinguished Prince.

...which he thought suited this purpose: so that those devoted to investigating this science would not be hindered by unfamiliar terminology <note>original: "insolenti nomenclatura"</note>. He then wrote concerning the <term>treatment of hard materials: "infectio" here refers to the chemical coloring or alteration of minerals to change their appearance</term> which are excavated from the veins of the earth. 

Whatever the quality of that writing may be, we have also published it so that we might expose the deception <note>original: "fucum," literally meaning "red dye," used here to mean "trickery" or "pretense"</note> of certain scoundrels who, through such a process, counterfeit gold, silver, and even every kind of copper coinage. 

To speak frankly, **LEONHARD PROEBSTEL** of Füssen—a man miraculously learned in <term>natural science: "Naturalium rerum cognitio," the study of the physical world</term> and mathematics <note>original: "Mathesi"</note>, and my dearest friend—had seriously warned me of this deceit. # JOHN GAR-
## LAND, THE ENGLISHMAN,
### a most learned philosopher: A Compendium of Alchemy, or: Commentaries on the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus <note>original Greek: *peri chēmeias*</note> concerning chemistry.

->*A PRAYER.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'L' featuring foliate patterns and a central figure or face within the ornamentation.</image-desc>

Praise, honor, virtue, and glory be to you, Lord God Almighty, together with your beloved Son, our Lord Jesus Christ, and the Holy Spirit, the Comforter. Holy Trinity—who are the only God and one, and perfect man <note>The author likely refers here to the dual nature of Christ within the Godhead.</note>—I give you thanks. Because when I had come to understand the fleeting nature of this world and its adversaries, your supreme mercy lifted me away from it, lest I be tempted by its pleasures. But because I see countless people deceived in this art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, which was often criticized for attracting fraudulent practitioners or those seeking only material wealth.</note>, failing to enter the direct path, may it please you, my Lord God, that I may lead my dear ones away from this error through the science <note>In this context, "science" (*scientia*) refers to organized knowledge or a specific discipline of study.</note> you have granted me. Thus, when they have perceived the truth, they may praise your holy and glorious name, which is blessed forever. Amen.

I indeed, **Hortulanus** <note>original: *Hortulanus*; Latin for "The Gardener." This is the pseudonym of a 14th-century alchemist whose commentary on the Emerald Tablet became one of the most influential texts in the Western alchemical tradition.</note>, named either from a garden or from a maritime fortress, wrapped in a **Jacobean skin** <note>original: *Jacobina pelle*; this likely refers to the black-and-white habit of the Dominican friars, who were often called "Jacobins" in France because their first convent was located on the Rue Saint-Jacques.</note> and the least of philosophers, unworthy to be called a disciple of philosophy: moved by love for my dearest friend, I shall declare the certain determination of the word of **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary "thrice-great" father of alchemical wisdom.</note>, the father of philosophers. This word, although hidden, I shall write with subtlety; however, through the work and labor of my fingers <note>A reference to practical laboratory work or "manual" alchemy as opposed to mere theory.</note>, I will declare to you the entire arrangement and the philosophers' practice of concealment in their words. For concealment is of no use where the teaching of the **Holy Spirit** operates.

### Chapter VI: That the stone is perfect if the soul has been fixed in the body.

Then it follows: *The father of every treasure of the whole world is here.* <note>original: "Pater omnis thesauri totius mundi est hic." A line from the Emerald Tablet, attributing the source of all alchemical power to this specific stage of the work.</note> This is because in the operation of the **stone** <term>Lapis: The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of alchemy.</term> there is one single, master operation, which the philosopher calls <gloss>Telesmi</gloss> <note>original: "Telesmi". This term is derived from the Greek *telesmos*, meaning "completion," "consecration," or "perfection." It refers to the final realization of the alchemical work.</note>. It is the "father of Telesmi"—that is, the father of every secret or treasure of the whole world, meaning every stone found in this world. *Is here*: It is as if he were saying, "Behold, I show it to you." Afterward, the philosopher says: "Do you want me to teach you when the strength of the stone is perfect and complete? It is when it has been turned into its own earth." And therefore he says, *Its power is complete if it has been turned into earth.*

The power of it (namely, the stone) is whole—that is, perfect and complete—if it has been turned into earth. That is to say, if the **soul** <term>Anima: In alchemy, the "soul" represents the volatile essence or life-force of a substance, often associated with the color red or the "oil."</term> of the stone itself—of which mention was made above—which soul is called "wind," which is **air** or **oil** <note>original: "aer, vel oleum". Alchemists often described the extracted essence of a substance as a "burning oil" or a subtle vapor.</note>, in which the whole strength of the stone resides, has been turned into earth. Then it becomes a **fixed stone** <term>Lapis fixus: A substance that has been "fixed" so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to high heat.</term>, so that the entire substance of the stone...

<summary>This page explains a key line of the Emerald Tablet, identifying the "father of all treasure" as the perfected alchemical process. It details how the "soul" or "wind" (volatile essence) must be "turned into earth" (solidified) to achieve the state of a "fixed stone."</summary>
<keywords>Emerald Tablet, Telesmi, soul, fixation, fixed stone, alchemical oil, perfection of the stone</keywords>

...[perfec]ted, it is fermented by its nurse—that is, by the earth—in such a way that the entire **stone** <term>Lapis: The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals and perfecting the human soul.</term> is converted into a **leaven** <term>Fermentum: In alchemy, "leaven" or "ferment" refers to a substance that, like yeast in bread, can transform and "ripen" a larger mass of material into its own nature.</term>. Just as in the preparation of bread, a small amount of leaven nourishes and ferments a very great quantity of bread, and in turn converts the whole substance of the dough into leaven, possessing that same power for another batch of dough. Thus the philosopher intends that our stone be fermented in this way, so that it might act as a leaven toward a multitude of other stones.

### *On the cleansing of the stone, Chapter VII.*

Then he explains how it ought to be multiplied; and first he establishes the **cleansing** <term>Mundificatio: The process of purification, specifically removing the "feces" or "shadow" from the elemental components.</term> of the stone and the separation of its parts, saying: *you shall separate the earth from the fire subtly, the subtle from the dense gently* <note>original: "separabis terram ab igne subtiliter, subtile à spisso suauiter." This is a core instruction from the Emerald Tablet, advising the alchemist to use gentle heat to separate volatile spirits from solid matter.</note>. This should be done with great skill—that is, gently and little by little, not by violence, but with ingenuity; which is to say, not in haste, but through great philosophy. You shall separate the dregs—that is, the impurities—from the fire, air, and water, and from the whole substance of the stone,

### Chapter VIII: That the non-fixed part of the stone should exceed the fixed part and elevate it.

Once the stone has been perfected in this way, it is ready for **multiplication** <term>Multiplicatio: The stage in the alchemical process where the power and quantity of the "Stone" are increased, allowing it to transform more base metal into gold.</term>. He then establishes the method of multiplication and its ease of melting through an all-pervading power, acting upon both hard and soft bodies, saying: *It ascends from the earth to the heaven, and again it descends to the earth* <note>original: "ascendit à terra in cælum, & iterum descendit in terram." This is a famous line from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, describing the circulation of the spirit through the material.</note>. 

It is very important to note here that although our stone is divided into four parts—which are the four elements, as mentioned previously—there are nevertheless two primary components. One is that which ascends to the top, which is called the **non-fixed** <term>Non fixa: Also called "volatile," this refers to the parts of the substance that vaporize or "fly" when heated.</term> part; the other is that which remains at the bottom, which is called the **fixed** <term>Fixa: The stable, solid part of the substance that remains at the bottom of the vessel and resists the fire.</term> part, or the "earth" or "leaven." As has been said, this fixed part nourishes and ferments the entire stone. 

Indeed, one must possess a great quantity of this non-fixed part, and apply it to the stone—which has been rendered most pure and free from filth—so many times

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the circulation of the stone's spirit, completing the discussion of Chapter VIII regarding the stone's "ascent" and beginning Chapter IX on its "descent" and final fixation.</meta>

...through the mastery <term>Magisterium: The "Great Work" or the completed alchemical process used to achieve the philosopher's stone.</term>, until it carries the entire stone upward to be **sublimated** <term>Sublimatio: A process where a substance is heated until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid, used here to purify and "spiritualize" the material.</term> and refined by the power of the non-fixed spirit. This is precisely what he means when he says: *It ascends from the earth to the heaven.* <note>original: "Ascēdit à terra in cælum." This refers to the purification of the matter as it rises as vapor in the vessel.</note>

->*How the volatile stone is to be fixed again. Chapter IX.*<-

Afterward, the stone that has been so exalted must be "waxed" <term>Inceratio: A process of adding a liquid "oil" or "spirit" to a dry substance until it reaches a soft, waxy consistency, making it easily melted or "fusible."</term> upon a marble slab with oil or with one of the elements extracted from the stone itself during the first operation—this is called the **water of the stone** <term>Aqua lapidis: A specialized alchemical solvent or "spirit" derived from the starting material, used to moisten the "earth" of the stone.</term>. It must then be "roasted" by sublimating it repeatedly until, by the power of the earth’s fermentation <term>Fermentatio: Just as leaven transforms dough into bread, the "ferment" or "earth" of the stone transforms the whole mass into its own nature.</term> (which has been exalted along with the "bodies" or solid parts), the entire stone once again descends from the heaven to the earth. At this point, it remains **fixed**—which is to say, it becomes **flowing** <note>original: "fixus id est, fluens." This is a classic alchemical paradox. A "fixed" stone is one that does not evaporate in the fire, but it must be "flowing" or "fusible" like wax so it can penetrate and transform other metals.</note>. 

And this is what he says: *And again it descends to the earth, and so it receives the power of superior things (through sublimation) and inferior things (through descent).* <note>original: "Iterumq; descendet in terram, & sic recipit uim superiorum scilicet sublimando, & inferiorum, scilicet descendendo." This completes the cycle of the Emerald Tablet's famous formula.</note> Thus, that which is bodily becomes spiritual during the ascent, and that which is spiritual becomes bodily during the descent.

### On the fruit of the art and the efficacy of the stone, Chapter X.

And thus you shall have the glory of the clarity <note>original: "claritatis." This means that all poverty and sickness shall depart, because the stone composed in this way is the healer <margin>* curable</margin> <note>The marginal note "curabilis" suggests that through the stone, all things are made "curable."</note> of every illness. 

And this is the strongest strength of all strength: for there is no comparison between this stone and the other strengths of this world. It will conquer—that is, by conquering it will transform **quicksilver** <term>Mercurium vivum: Literally "living mercury," the raw liquid metal which alchemists sought to "fix" or solidify.</term> by congealing it, despite its subtle <note>In this context, "subtle" refers to a substance that is volatile, spirit-like, or easily evaporated.</note> nature. It will also penetrate other hard and solid bodies, even those made of iron, because it overcomes every subtle thing and penetrates every solid thing.

### Why the stone is animal. Chapter XV.

That it is also animal, I, who speak, know; for when the elements of the **Mercury** itself are separated and mixed back together in equal weight, with nothing else added, in a strong glass vessel with a small vent, and placed in soft dung at a gentle heat, within three months horrible worms are birthed. One of these devours the others until only one remains, which feeds upon **living Mercury**. <term>Living Mercury: also known as quicksilver or *mercurius vivus*, representing the fluid, transformative principle of the work.</term> This grows to the size of a toad, whose form is terrifying, and this animal is itself the **Elixir**. <note>The "Toad" is a famous alchemical symbol for the heavy, earthy, and often poisonous "fixed" matter that must be purified; its ability to consume its own kind represents the process of unification.</note> Applied to lead, the stone is therefore animal.

Note that here the **philosopher** <note>This likely refers to an authority like Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus, frequently cited in alchemical texts</note> defines the **stone** <note>original: *lapidem*. And because the spirit does not appear to us, nor can it be touched unless it is a body, this spirit—by the nobility of its nature—took on a **living body** in the noblest of the elements, namely in the **sphere of fire** <term>sphere of fire: in the medieval geocentric model, the region of the atmosphere composed of the element fire, located just below the orbit of the moon</term>, remaining in its spiritual nature: nor for this reason is it fire, nor does it have the nature of fire as far as it is in itself. It is true that it is a burning spiritual body, on account of the subtlety in its purity, and because of this we cannot see it. ### How the Stone is to be Made — Chapter XX.

# MASTER ARNOLD OF
### VILLANOVA, THE CELEBRATED
### Philosopher, his Exposition
### on the Commentaries
### of Hortulanus.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring a seated figure, possibly a scholar or alchemist, surrounded by foliage and architectural elements.</image-desc>

WHAT are the species <note>original: *species*; here referring to the specific types of substances or forms of matter</note> that come together in the composition of the **Elixir**? **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: *Argentum vivum*, or mercury; in alchemy, it represents the volatile, feminine, and "watery" principle of matter</term>, and **fleeing yellow sulfur** <term>fleeing: *fugiens*, meaning volatile or easily evaporated by heat</term>, **fixed green sulfur**—which has a **black belly** <note>original: *ventrem nigrum*; a traditional alchemical metaphor suggesting a hidden impurity or a stage of "darkness" (nigredo) contained within the substance</note>, even though it appears clear—and **fixed white sulfur**. These are the three sulfurs. They are flowing and melting, just like wax: they are better when new than when old. Good **green sulfur** is that which, when broken, has a clear and bright fracture like glass, and is found in large pieces. **Fixed white sulfur** is better when it is thoroughly white, shining in its fracture, and

**Fleeing yellow sulfur** <term>fleeing: *fugiens*, referring to its volatility or tendency to evaporate when heated</term> is **living sulfur** <note>original: *sulphur vivum*; native, unrefined sulfur found in nature</note>. It is better when it is found in large chunks and is not stony.
Moreover, **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: *argentum vivum*, or mercury</term> is combined with **yellow sulfur** in such a way that they are transformed and become one in a single bright, red, and heavy mass <note>This "red mass" refers to cinnabar (mercury sulfide), which alchemists created by heating mercury and sulfur together</note>. Once these have been obtained, take one pound of twelve ounces of **fixed green sulfur** and grind it upon marble, or elsewhere, and make a very fine powder, which you should keep by itself.
Second, take three ounces of **fixed white sulfur** and grind it just like the other, and keep it by itself to the side.
Third, take three ounces—that is, a fourth part of a pound—of the **earth** composed of **sulfur** and **Mercury**, and grind it until nothing bright appears within it, and keep it by itself to the side. This composition, made from the three aforementioned substances, is called the **Blessed Stone** <note>original: *lapis benedictus*</note>, which is mineral, animal, and vegetable, because it does not have a single proper name. It is called **Mineral**, because the things from which the medicine is composed are minerals; **Vegetable**, because it becomes alive and grows; and **Animal**, because it possesses a soul, a spirit, and a body, just like living animals. 

By another name, it is called the **stinking black** <note>original: *nigrum foetens*</note>, because it has a black "belly" and is composed of a stinking spirit. In another way, it is called **Chaos**, or the **origin of the world**, or the **confused mass** <note>original: *massa confusa*; referring to the primal state of matter before it is organized</note>; we, however, call it **earth**.

Let the fire be so tempered <note>The author emphasizes a gentle, regulated heat—hot enough to work, but cool enough to touch.</note> that you can hold your hand against the base of the heating pot inside the iron plate. The fire must be continuous, because if it were unstable at the beginning, the matter would melt within the vessel before the **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*; the volatile, gaseous part of the substance</note> could fly away from it; the vessel would then break, the spirit of the **fifth essence** <term>fifth essence: *quinta essentia*; the purest, most medicinal part of the matter, separated from the four physical elements</term> would escape, and the whole work would be lost. 

Therefore, this fire must be maintained with great diligence. ...than the preceding. A **gentle fire** <note>original: *ignis debilis*; a low, steady heat used in the initial stages of distillation to prevent the glass from cracking</note> must be continued for one day and night, or thereabouts; afterward, it must be strengthened little by little. This increase in heat should be maintained until the **alembic** <term>alembic: *alembicus*; the head or cap of the distilling apparatus where vapors condense</term> glows red. 

When it has appeared red, the fire must be held in that state until all the **water** <note>original: *aqua*; in this context, it refers to the liquid distillate being extracted</note> has come out, or nearly so. Thirdly, the fire must be strengthened again, this time with an open flame for six hours, until all the **strong and thick water** <note>original: *aqua fortis & spissa*; the more concentrated, viscous substance that emerges only under intense heat</note> has come out, and the **dry earth** <note>original: *terra sicca*; the solid residue, also known as the "dead head," left at the bottom of the vessel</note> remains without moisture. 

Then the **Blessed Water** <note>original: *aqua Benedicta*; a term for a highly purified alchemical solvent believed to have transformative properties</note> will be complete. This is called the **Water of Mercury and of Sulfur** <note>original: *aqua Mercurij... & sulphuris*</note>, because it is generated from them. By another name, it is called smoke, wind, oil, water, air, watery oil, airy oil, fire, life, soul, spirit, and **Mercury** <term>Mercury: *Mercurius*; here used philosophically to describe the "spirit" or volatile essence of the matter</term>. 

It is a burning fire and dissolves all bodies by a single regimen—that is, by one process—namely the work of the following **Autumn** <note>original: *Autumni*; alchemical stages were often metaphorically linked to the seasons of the year</note>. Likewise, it is called the **Blessed Stone** by the philosophers, though it is not a stone, nor does it have the nature <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to explain that the "Stone" is a spiritual or liquid substance rather than a literal rock.</note>...

<summary>This page details the three stages of increasing heat required to complete the distillation of the "Blessed Water" and lists its many symbolic names, such as the Water of Mercury and the Blessed Stone.</summary>
<keywords>distillation, gentle fire, alembic, Blessed Water, Mercury, Sulfur, Blessed Stone, alchemical nomenclature, dissolution</keywords>

It is not fire, nor does it have a fiery nature in and of itself, yet it dwells within fire. 

And because this fire in its own sphere <note>In the medieval worldview, the "sphere of fire" was the region above the air and below the moon where the purest element of fire resided</note> cannot be seen or touched by us, it is converted into the substance of water within suitable instruments through the industry of the operation, by compounding and condensing, as has been said. 

In this water are the four elements; but three of them—namely water, air, and fire—are for the composition of these three elements with the fourth, namely with earth. impregnation and a true union <note>original: "coitus"; used here to describe the chemical conjunction of substances symbolized as a sexual union of male and female principles.</note>. These four bodies are extracted clouds and snows, the volatile part <note>original: "volitum"; likely referring to the substance that has been turned into vapor or sublimated.</note>, and butter; "moon-spit" <note>original: "Lunae spuma"; a common alchemical name for a white, frothy precipitate or "white mercury."</note> or spirit. 

Likewise, it is called the "leaven of the stone" for both [the white and red works], and "black lead," and our whole operation, and the operation of all philosophers, and of all "hens" <note>original: "gallinarum"; an unusual metaphor likely referring to the "brooding" or slow, gentle heat of the furnace, similar to a hen incubating her eggs.</note>, and our wisdom. As soon as you have placed the waters in the <term>gourd<note>original: "cucurbita"; a glass vessel with a rounded bottom used for digestion and distillation.</note></term>, cover the gourd so that none of the spirit of the quintessence escapes, until they have been joined and become one single thing—namely, a red and clear water. These two seeds are necessary in this art; its father is the Sun and its mother is the Moon <note>This is a direct reference to the *Emerald Tablet*, a famous ancient text which claims the Philosopher's Stone is born of the Sun (gold/sulfur) and Moon (silver/mercury).</note>. They are necessary because the Sun and Moon are

<summary>This page lists the various symbolic names for the alchemical "water" (the combined Elixir) and emphasizes the need to seal the vessel to preserve the volatile spirit during the union of the solar and lunar seeds.</summary>
<keywords>quintessence, magnesia, Moon-spittle, leaven of the stone, gourd, Sun and Moon, alchemical marriage, seeds</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical "marriage" of substances, specifically focusing on the melting properties of the mixture and the symbolic familial roles of the elements involved.</meta>

are difficult to melt on their own, but when joined together they flow and liquefy easily. <term>Goldsmiths<note>original: "aurifabri"</note></term> know this well, and therefore this mixing was necessary. The final secret is to possess a <term>medicine<note>In alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the transformative substance or tincture capable of "curing" base metals into gold or perfecting the human body.</note></term> that flows before the <term>quicksilver<note>original: "Mercurium vivum"; liquid mercury, which is highly volatile and evaporates quickly when heated.</note></term> can evaporate and fly away from the fire. For this reason, take care that the spirit of the <term>quintessence<note>The "fifth essence" or purest spiritual force within matter, believed to be the life-force that transcends the four physical elements.</note></term> does not escape; it is held back by this union, and thus the work shall be completed, God willing.

When these two waters are joined, the Blessed Stone is impregnated "in the belly of the wind" <note>This is a famous reference to the Emerald Tablet, an ancient foundational text of alchemy, suggesting the substance exists in a gaseous or spiritual state before it solidifies.</note>; the "nurse" of this water is the earth. Note that the mother and father of the stone are called the <term>Sun and Moon<note>original: "Sol & Luna"; symbolic names for gold and silver, or the masculine (sulfur) and feminine (mercury) principles.</note></term> during the mixing of the <term>Elixir<note>The "Elixir" here refers to the transitional stage of the substance before it reaches its final state as the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term>, but later in the process of the stone, they are called the "earth" or the "nurse."

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical components must be combined to lower their melting point and prevent the volatile "spirit" from escaping. It uses symbolic language from the Emerald Tablet to describe the Stone's development as an embryo nourished by the earth.</summary>

# OF JOHN GAR-
### LAND, THE ENGLISH PHILO-
#### sopher, most learned; an exposition 
#### of Synonyms in the 
#### Alchemical art.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring a satyr-like figure on the left and foliage or a creature on the right.</image-desc>

**AMATURA**, that
is, the dark stone <note>original: "petra maura"; likely referring to a type of magnetite or "Moorish stone" used in early mineralogy.</note>.
**Atramentum**, that is,
vitriol <term>Vitriol<note>A sulfate of metal, such as iron or copper. "Atramentum" literally means "ink-black," as these minerals were primary ingredients in making ink.</note></term>.
**Alkali**, that is, calci-
nation in <note>original: "calcitura in"; likely referring to the ashes produced by calcining plants or minerals to produce alkaline salts.</note>.
**Aludel**, that is, an earthenware vessel <term>Aludel<note>A specialized pear-shaped clay pot, often open at both ends, used as a condenser in the process of sublimation.</note></term>.
**Almager**, that is, red ochre <note>original: "synopide rubeum"; a red pigment containing iron oxide, often called Sinopia.</note>,
resembling grains.
**Antimonium**, that is, black kohl <note>original: "alcophil nigra"; a reference to "al-kuhl," the fine black powder of stibnite used for eye makeup and alchemical experiments.</note>.
**Alcharit**, that is, quicksilver <term>Quicksilver<note>Liquid mercury; "Alcharit" is a Latinized version of an Arabic alchemical term.</note></term>.
**Almisadir** & **Mixadir**, that is, sal Am-
moniac <term>Sal Ammoniac<note>Ammonium chloride, a volatile salt highly valued in alchemy for its ability to clean metals and aid in the "spiritization" of matter.</note></term>.
**Arsenicum**, that is, orpiment <term>Orpiment<note>Arsenic trisulfide, a yellow mineral whose Latin name "auripigmentum" means "gold pigment," used to simulate or create a golden color.</note></term>.
**Alcali**, that is, molten ink <note>Likely referring to a metallic sulfate or vitriol that has been liquefied or dissolved.</note>.
**Aremaros**, that is, cinnabar <note>The bright red ore of mercury, known as mercuric sulfide.</note>.
**Almarcech**, that is, litharge <term>Litharge<note>One of the natural mineral forms of lead monoxide, often produced as a byproduct during the refining of silver from lead ores.</note></term>.

<summary>This page provides a glossary of Arabic-derived alchemical terms, identifying them as common laboratory substances like sal ammoniac, mercury, lead, and various salts. It highlights the heavy influence of Arabic science on the Latin alchemical tradition.</summary>
<keywords>sal ammoniac, mercury, quicksilver, alkali, camphor, white lead, copper oxide, Azoth, alchemical glossary, Arabic terms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of alchemical and technical terms, maintaining the focus on the letter 'A'. It follows the previous page’s pattern of defining Arabic and technical Latin terms through more familiar substances. This section is particularly rich in various types of "alums" (mineral salts) and natural pigments.</meta>

**Liquid alum**, that is, **amomum** <note>original: "amomi"; likely referring to an oily or resinous mineral extract similar in appearance to the spice amomum.</note>
**Phaseolum alum**, that is, **alkali** <note>original: "alkali"; a basic substance derived from the ashes of burnt plants.</note>
**Creped alum**, that is, **tartar of good wine** (shredded) <note>original: "tartarũ boni vini (scissi)"; the acidic crust that forms inside wine casks, used as a flux in alchemy.</note>
**Feather alum**, that is, **Yemeni** or **cleft** [alum] <note>original: "iameni vel scissi"; likely a fibrous mineral like asbestos or a specific crystalline form of alum from Yemen.</note>
**Alexandrian alum**, that is, **false nitre** <note>original: "nitrum falsum"; nitre usually refers to saltpeter, but "false nitre" may indicate a mineral that mimics its cooling properties or appearance.</note>
**Goat alum**, that is, **alkali salt** <note>original: "sal alkali"; potentially a specific animal-processed salt or a variant of caustic soda.</note>
**Clay-like**, that is, **glazed**. <note>original: "Argillata... vitreata"; describing a material that has been given a glassy or ceramic coating.</note>
**Asafoetida** <note>A pungent resinous gum from the Ferula plant, valued for its medicinal and transformative properties.</note>
**Split alum**, that is, **annacos** <note>original: "annacos"; an Arabic-derived term for a fragmented mineral salt.</note>
**A type of black pigment**, that is, **dominenec** <note>original: "Atramẽti species"; *atramentum* was a general term for vitriol or any substance used to make black ink.</note>
**White alum**, that is, **elemine fab** <note>original: "elemine fab"; possibly referring to a resin like elemi or a specific purified mineral.</note>
**Azure**, that is, **eleruad** <note>original: "Azur... eleruad"; lapis lazuli or the blue pigment derived from it.</note>
**Asphalt**, that is, **Jewish bitumen** <note>original: "bitumen Iudaicũ"; a natural mineral pitch found in the Dead Sea region, used in embalming and medicine.</note>
**Armenian stone**, that is, **bole** <note>original: "Armenium... bolum"; a medicinal earth or clay from Armenia, often reddish, used as an astringent.</note>
**Amalgra**, that is, **Cypriot bole** <note>original: "bolum ciprinum"; a colored clay from Cyprus, frequently used in the preparation of gold or as a pigment.</note>
**Alet**, that is, **salt** <note>original: "sal".</note>
**Alaferangi**, that is, **the washing of burnt lead** <note>original: "lotura plumbi vsti"; the residue obtained by washing lead that has been oxidized by fire.</note>
**Acureb**, that is, **glass** <note>original: "vitrum".</note>
**Aycafor**, that is, **burnt copper** <note>original: "æs vstum"; copper oxide created by heating copper plates until they flake.</note>
**Annatum**, that is, **eggshells** <note>original: "testa ouorum"; powdered eggshells were a common source of calcium in chemical recipes.</note>
**Adebessi**, that is, **a tortoise** <note>original: "testudo"; likely referring to the shell of a turtle or tortoise used in powdered preparations.</note>
**Ammoniac**, that is, **gensir** <note>original: "Armoniacus... gensir"; either gum ammoniac or sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride).</note>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of alchemical and technical terms, moving from the conclusion of the letter 'A' into the beginning of the letter 'B'. **Ink**, or vitriol, that is, **melagistico** <note>original: "melagistico"; likely derived from the Greek 'melan' for black, referring here to substances like copperas or green vitriol used to make ink.</note>
**Agrabi**, that is, **shredded**, or **cold** <note>original: "raci, vel frigida"; 'Agrabi' is a transliteration of an Arabic term, possibly relating to a cooling medicinal property.</note>
**Quicksilver**, that is, **of the dew** <note>original: "de rossero"; alchemists frequently used the poetic metaphor of "celestial dew" to describe the shimmering, liquid nature of mercury.</note>
**Adamant**, that is, **helsmela** <note>original: "helsmela"; 'Adamant' was a general term for exceptionally hard stones, including diamonds and emery.</note>
**Alums, alofor**, that is, **rali** <note>original: "alofor... rali"; these are likely various grades of mineral salts used as mordants in dyeing.</note>
**Liquid alum**, that is, **clear** <note>original: "limparum"; referring to a purified or dissolved form of the mineral.</note>
**White alum**, that is, **lentil-shaped** <note>original: "lentica"; describing the physical appearance of the alum crystals.</note>
**Alcmabunen**, that is, **Luberi** <note>original: "Luberi"; likely a corruption of 'olibanum', referring to frankincense resin.</note>
**Baurat**, that is, **every kind of salt** <note>original: "omne genus salis"; derived from the Arabic 'bauraq', used for both borax and natron.</note>
**Sea-shells**, the same <note>original: "cepta marina idein"; likely referring to burnt shells used for their alkaline lime content.</note>
**Ibocta with poison**. <note>original: "Ibocta cum veneno"; 'ibocta' is an obscure term, potentially referring to a toxic mineral compound like arsenic.</note>
**Copper scales**, that is, **copper flakes** <note>original: "tubeta æris"; the shavings or "hammer-scales" produced when working copper, used as a base for pigments and chemical reactions.</note>
**Bauret**, that is, **borax** <note>original: "boraco"; a flux used in soldering and metallurgy.</note>
**Bolus**, that is, **the sediment of vitriol** <note>original: "sedina atramenti"; while 'bolus' often refers to medicinal clay, here it describes the residue left over from processing black pigments.</note>
**Bliacta**, that is, **red earth** <note>original: "terra rubea"; a type of iron-rich clay used as a pigment and in medicine.</note>
**Brazilwood**, that is, **shavings** <note>original: "bractan"; a wood from which a popular red dye was extracted in the Middle Ages.</note>
**Boleson**, that is, **Balsam**.
**To take a mouthful**, that is, **to feed**.
**Bacar**, that is, **weight** <note>original: "pondus"; likely a specific unit of measure or a term for gravity/heaviness.</note>
**Bitrinati**, that is, **glazed** <note>original: "vitreatum"; referring to materials that have been given a glassy or ceramic coating.</note>
**Bezoar**, that is, **green stone** <note>original: "lapis viridis"; although usually an animal gallstone, in alchemy, 'bezoar' often referred to any stone believed to act as a powerful antidote.</note>
**Bolesis**, that is, **coral**.
**Jewish Bolus**, that is, **al hea** <note>original: "al hea"; a specific medicinal clay, or 'bolus armenus', found in the Levant.</note>
**Golden halter**, that is, **Borax** <note>original: "Capistrum auri"; a poetic alchemical name for borax because it "binds" or "leads" the gold during the melting and soldering process.</note>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical dictionary of alchemical and technical synonyms, focusing on terms beginning with the letters E, F, and G. **Es**, that is, **the body** <note>original: "corpus"; in alchemy, the "body" usually refers to the physical, metallic form of a substance.</note>

**Phebus**, that is, **virgin boy** <note>original: "puer virgo"; Phoebus is the sun god, often symbolizing gold in alchemy; "virgin boy" is an allegorical name for a pure, unworked substance.</note>
**GAzar**, that is, **laurel** <note>original: "laurus"; the bay laurel tree.</note>

**Kist**, that is, **opopanax** <note>original: "opopanax"; a pungent medicinal gum resin.</note>
**Karabe**, that is, **gum like fofarata** <note>original: "gumma sicut fofarata"; "Karabe" is a term for amber, and "fofarata" likely refers to a specific processed state or a related resin.</note>

**Laum**, that is, **bitter almond** <note>original: "amigdala amara".</note>
**Laos**, that is, **tin** <note>original: "stagnum".</note>
**Armenian mud**, that is, **bole** <note>original: "Lutum armen... bolus"; Armenian bole is a reddish clay used in medicine and as a gilding base.</note>
**Latune**, that is, **silvery earth** <note>original: "terra argentea".</note>
**Poppy milk**, that is, **opium** <note>original: "Lac papaueris"; the dried latex harvested from the opium poppy.</note>
**Luben**, that is, **frankincense** <note>original: "thus"; the aromatic resin used in incense and ancient medicine.</note>
**Latro**, that is, **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum viuum"; the liquid metal mercury.</note>

**Lamere**, that is, **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphur".</note>
**Later**, that is, **performed** <note>original: "perpetrata"; while "Later" usually means a brick, here it is glossed as something completed or enacted.</note>

**Melc**, that is, **sour milk** <note>original: "lac acetosum".</note>
**Macra**, that is, **red chalk** <note>original: "creta rubea"; red ochre or ruddle, used as a pigment.</note>
**Messellum**, that is, **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iupiter"; the alchemical code name for the metal **tin**.</note>
**Moz**, that is, **myrrh** <note>original: "mirra".</note>
**Mars**, that is, **Venus** <note>original: "Mars, id est Venus"; a rare symbolic substitution where the traditionally masculine metal (iron) is equated with the feminine (copper).</note>
**Madic**, that is, **cow's milk** <note>original: "lac vaccinum".</note>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical glossary of technical and alchemical terms, moving from the letter M through P. **Minium**, that is, **cinnabar** <note>original: "cinabrium"; in medieval texts, these terms were often used interchangeably for red pigments, though minium is technically red lead and cinnabar is mercury sulfide.</note>

**Osatur**, that is, **oregano** <note>original: "organum"; wild marjoram.</note>
**Oscieum**, that is, **celery** <note>original: "apium"; also used for wild parsley.</note>
**Palestinian oil**, that is, **vinegar** <note>original: "acetum"; likely an alchemical "deck-name" to disguise the use of vinegar.</note>
**Oabelcora**, that is, **a cucurbit** <note>original: "cucurbita"; a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used in distillation.</note>
**Pelude**, that is, **cooked honey** <note>original: "mel coctum".</note>
**Phibit**, that is, **predatory** <note>original: "rapax"; possibly a term for a "hungry" or corrosive substance.</note>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new treatise after the conclusion of the alphabetical glossary on the preceding pages. It introduces a practical alchemical handbook attributed to the 13th-century English scholar John Garland (Joannes de Garlandia), shifting the focus from definitions to the specific procedures for creating the "Philosophers' Stone."</meta>

# JOHN GAR-
## LAND, THE ENGLISH PHILO-
### sopher, most learned, on the prep-
### aration of the Elixir, a lit-
### tle book.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'O' featuring a scholar or alchemist seated at a desk with a book, surrounded by architectural and floral motifs.</image-desc>

A most noble and brief work for composing the **Elixir**, or the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: "lapidem philosophicum"; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life</note>. It is achieved, if the mastery is well-guided, in four months, provided the "body" is red. If, however, the spirit is joined with a white body, it will be the medicine or Elixir for the white <note>referring to the *albedo*, the second major stage of the alchemical process, associated with silver</note>. **Sublimated mercury** <term>mercury that has been heated into vapor and collected as a solid crust</term> will be revivified <note>original: "viuificabitur"; the process of restoring solid mercury back to its liquid, "living" metallic state</note> only slightly; however, if the mercury is revivified, it will cause all the white mercury to be revivified. Therefore, the first operation is that you sublime mercury with **Roman vitriol** <term>naturally occurring iron or copper sulfate</term>, or **copperas** <term>green vitriol or ferrous sulfate</term>, until it is thoroughly "mortified" <note>original: "mortificatus"; a term for chemically altering a metal—usually through oxidation or calcination—so that it loses its metallic luster and fluidity</note>, so that there is one part mercury and [one part] vitriol...

<summary>This page serves as the title and introduction to John Garland's alchemical treatise on the Philosophers' Stone, outlining the timeline for the work and the initial preparation of mercury.</summary>
<keywords>John Garland, Philosophers' Stone, Elixir, mercury, sublimation, vitriol, alchemical stages, mortification</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the first operation of the elixir, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's recipe for mortifying mercury. It then transitions into the second major stage of the process involving the union of mercury and gold.</meta>

...another part of **copperas** <term>also known as green vitriol; a sulfate of iron or copper</term>, or if you wish, you may use two parts of copperas or **vitriol**. After the **mercury** has been thoroughly mortified <note>original: "mortificatus"; a term for chemically altering a metal—usually through oxidation or calcination—so that it loses its metallic luster and fluidity</note>, then take the said **sublimated mercury** <term>mercury that has been heated into vapor and collected as a solid crust</term> and pulverize it well. Mix it with pulverized vitriol and sublime it from the aforesaid vitriol seven times, ensuring there is as much vitriol as there is sublimated mercury. At each turn, however, fresh vitriol must be mixed with the aforementioned sublimated mercury. This shall be the **first operation**, which is called the **operation of the spirit**.

The **second operation** follows, which is this: take equal parts <note>original: "ana"; a common alchemical and medical term from Greek meaning 'of each'</note> of the aforesaid seven-times-sublimated mercury and the purest **sun** <term>sun (sol): the alchemical term for gold</term> which has been perfectly calcined <term>reduced to a powder through intense heat</term>. When these two similar things have been mixed and incorporated upon a marble slab, place them in a round glass flask or ampoule. Close the opening or mouth of the vessel with the **lute of wisdom** <term>luto sapientiæ: a heavy, airtight sealant made of clay, dung, and other materials, designed to withstand high heat</term>. Then, let it be placed in a weak **ash-fire** <note>original: "igne cinerum"; a gentle, indirect method of heating where the glass vessel is buried in a bed of warm ashes</note>, and let it stand there for...

<summary>This page details the repetitive sublimation of mercury with vitriol to complete the "operation of the spirit" and describes the start of the second operation: combining this purified mercury with calcined gold in a sealed vessel.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, mercury, vitriol, copperas, operation of the spirit, gold, calcination, lute of wisdom, ash-fire, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the second operation of the elixir, which involves the heating and circulation of mercury and gold in an ash-fire. It picks up from the previous page's instruction to bury the glass vessel in ashes for a period of time.</meta>

...for nine days at the least. Using the said **ampoule** <term>a small, rounded glass flask</term>, let the ashes be buried over it in a small quantity; for it is enough simply that it be covered by the ashes. Leave it thus under the aforementioned ashes until you see that all the **Mercury** has ascended to the top of the sides of the said **round flask** <note>original: "rotumbæ"; a medieval Latin variant for a spherical or round-bottomed vessel</note>. Then resolve it <note>original: "resolue"; in this context, it refers to letting the vaporized mercury settle, condense, or melt back down to the bottom</note>, and make it so that the **calcined body** <note>the gold powder described on the previous page</note> falls, or rests upon the **Mercury**. Then once more leave the said round flask in the same manner under the warm ashes.

And you should know that all the **Mercury** usually ascends within twelve hours. When the nine days have finished, apply a stronger fire, so that whatever can be **sublimated** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again in a cooler part of the vessel</term> may ascend and be sublimated. Once this is done, empty the vessel with caution, lest the sublimated material be mixed with the material that remained in the lower part of the aforementioned round flask.

The **third operation** follows, which is that you should take

<summary>This page describes the final steps of the second alchemical operation, where mercury and gold are heated together to induce sublimation over a nine-day period. The process focuses on the repeated "ascent" of mercury and its careful separation from the remaining residue.</summary>

<keywords>Mercury, gold, sublimation, ampoule, round flask, ash-fire, calcination, third operation</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the final steps of the second alchemical operation. The previous page ended with instructions to empty the vessel carefully after the sublimation of mercury and gold. This page details the final refinement of the material and the resulting transmuting "medicine."</meta>

Take what has been **sublimated** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid</term> in the manner described before. As for that which remains at the bottom, it is of no concern; however, for the sake of the work, you could reduce it <note>the residue</note> back into a "body" <note>likely meaning a solid metal state</note>, and it will be as good as, or perhaps even better, than it was before.

And place that same **sublimated** material into a **round flask** <note>original: "rotumba"; a spherical glass vessel</note>, which you should place over a slow fire as before. Rotate and circulate it just as you did above, and keep it circulating until the material that was sublimated there falls drop by drop back down. This process will take one month, or thirty days, or forty at the most.

When you see that the material has fallen to the bottom of the **round flask** and will no longer sublimate any higher, then leave the flask with that same material over the same weak fire for another thirty days. Then, the **medicine** <term>medicine: the alchemical elixir or transmuting agent</term> will remain, fused into a single fragile mass.

*The fifth operation or the fifth part of the work follows, in which the method for the **projection** <term>proiectionis: the final stage where the "medicine" or Philosopher's Stone is applied to base metals to transform or "perfect" them</term> of the **medicine** is set down.*

In the same way, one part of this brittle mass, when placed upon ten parts of Mercury, will congeal it into a brittle mass just as before. This will be the medicine for congealing Mercury, and by this method, you will be able to congeal Mercury infinitely, for it will always remain a medicine for congealing Mercury.

The sixth part of the work follows, in which is taught how one must proceed to the **rubification** <term>rubificationem: "reddening," the final stage of the alchemical process where the substance reaches its highest degree of maturity and potency</term> of the **medicine**; proceed, therefore, in this manner: 

Take one part of **Gold** <term>Solis: literally "of the Sun," the standard alchemical name for gold</term>, and another part of the said **congealed Mercury**, which you shall mix together and place in a **reverberatory furnace** <term>furno reuerberationis: a furnace where the heat is reflected or "reverberated" from the roof onto the material being heated</term> to be **calcined** <term>calcinandum: the process of reducing a substance to a powder or "ash" through high heat</term> for a natural day <note>A "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour cycle of day and night</note>. Then the material will become calcined like a **red ash**; but if the calcination is not complete, let it be done so that the proper sign appears. This being done, let the said red ash be dissolved in our **sharp water** <term>aqua noſtra acuta: a potent corrosive or acid, likely a mineral acid used for dissolution</term>, in the manner we have set down above, and afterwards let it be congealed. 

Then take one part of this kind of congealed material, and another part of the **medicine** described above which melts easily like tin; let these be ground together and **imbibed** <term>imbibantur: a process of slowly adding liquid to a dry substance until it is saturated</term> with that clean, volatile part that was dissolved in vinegar or in our **sharp water**, until it melts very easily, first cooking the **amalgam** <term>amalgamam: a mixture of mercury and another metal, in this case gold</term> in a **water bath** <term>balneo: shorthand for "balneum Mariae" or the "bath of Mary," a vessel of hot water used for gentle, indirect heating</term>

# THE BOOK ON MINERALS
### BY JOHN OF GARLAND, THE MOST LEARNED
### ENGLISH PHILOSOPHER.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'R' featuring a figure (possibly a scholar or alchemist) standing amidst foliage and a dragon-like creature.</image-desc>

THE vital principle <note>original: "Ratio"; here meaning the organizing essence or governing proportion</note> found in living creatures, in the **spirits** of minerals, and in various forms of **arsenic** <note>original: "arsenicis"; in medieval alchemy, this usually referred to arsenic sulfides like orpiment (yellow) and realgar (red)</note>, is the "soul" of the substance, and it is mineral in nature. Its nature is hot and moist <note>Referring to the four primary qualities of Aristotelian physics which determined a substance's temperament</note>, and it appears in two colors: namely, red and pale. 

The arsenic that we recommend is pale and flaky <gloss>foliatum: leaf-like or layered in structure</gloss>, and this type of arsenic is more potent than all others, yet it is not so destructive as to burn through all other substances. Indeed, arsenic is very similar to **sulfur** <note>original: "sulphuri"; along with mercury, sulfur was considered one of the two fundamental components of all metals</note> in many alchemical operations, but it is more refined than sulfur, both in the speed of its **dissolution** <gloss>solutionis: the process of breaking down a solid into liquid form</gloss> and in its "patience"—or endurance—under the heat of the fire. 

The pale variety is more resistant to fire than the red; the pale also **tints** <gloss>tingit: the alchemical "tincturing" or dyeing of a metal to change its appearance and nature</gloss> better, and its final results are superior. Regarding its properties...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the formation of metals, contrasting nature’s slow timeline with the alchemist’s ability to accelerate the process. It focuses on the four methods of preparation, specifically the theory and practice of calcination as taught by the master Geber.</meta>

**gold** <note>original: *aurum*</note> and **silver** <note>original: *argentum*</note> in a thousand years. If these bodies had remained in the mines, nature would work upon them until they became silver and gold. But God placed wisdom within this noble **microcosm** <term>microcosm: *minori mundo*; literally "lesser world," referring here to the alchemist's laboratory or the human art that mirrors the "macrocosm" of the vast universe</term>—which is created in the image of the greater world—so that it can transform these bodies into silver and gold in a single day. They called this the **sulfur of the earth** <note>original: *sulphur terræ*</note>, and their preparation is done in four ways.

The first is **calcination** <term>calcination: *calcinamentum*; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures, typically in a furnace, to reduce it to a brittle, powdery state</term> with salt, followed by **liquefaction** <note>melting</note> and then **coagulation** <note>solidifying or thickening</note>. But calcination is their burning with a strong fire along with salt, so that the **sulfur**—which caused corruption within them—is removed from them. They then remain pure, like a **calx**, and indeed, it is calx. 

**Geber** <note>the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century scholar regarded as the father of chemistry</note> said that these bodies required calcination so that their parts might be cleansed, and so they would be ready for the penetration of **moisture** <note>original: *humiditatis*</note>. This allows the moisture to penetrate deeply, which then has the power to dissolve them. By strengthening their dryness, they will become more receptive to moisture, and thus better suited for **tinting** <note>original: *ad tingendum*; the final alchemical stage of "coloring" a base metal to give it the properties of gold or silver</note>.

<summary>This page explains that while nature takes a thousand years to form gold and silver in mines, the alchemist can achieve this quickly through "wisdom." It details the process of calcination with salt to remove corrupting sulfur and prepare metals for dissolution and tinting.</summary>

Place earth over the mouth of the vessel and sand over the earth, and leave it for a day and a night; then it will be dissolved, if God wills.

### On the Same Subject, Chapter VIII.

Take the best gold, as much as you wish, and make it into leaves. Heat these leaves and quench <note>original: *extinguas*; to rapidly cool a hot metal in liquid</note> them in salt water <note>original: *aqua salis*; a brine solution</note>, and afterwards in another water in which lead was melted for so long that it turned black. Then grind these leaves until they are well pulverized, so much so that you cannot even feel them with your fingers, and afterwards work with it, God willing <note>original: *Deo annuente*</note>.

->Another [method].<-

Take some **almarchach** <note>original: *almarchach*; an Arabic-derived term for litharge</note>, that is, the stone litharge <term>litharge: *litargyro*; lead monoxide, a byproduct of silver refining often used as a flux</term> in which silver is melted; grind it and mix it into a mass with oil. Place it in a goldsmith’s cupel <term>goldsmith’s cupel: *testa aurifabri*; a shallow, porous vessel used in the process of refining precious metals</term> until the surface of the melt is covered. Then take gold filings and place them in an **Albot** <term>Albot: a specialized alchemical vessel or crucible used for melting or calcination</term> and melt them. When it has cooled, grind it and keep it hidden until you wish to use it.

### Chapter 11. On Iron.

The nature of iron. Iron is of the parts of Mars <note>alchemists associated the seven known metals with the seven celestial bodies; Mars, the god of war, was the ruler of iron</note>, and its nature is hot and dry—though some have called it cold and dry. It is masculine and feminine in taste, and it is the source of strength, enduring fire and yet fighting with fire. It is dissolved by four things: namely arsenic, lead, magnesia <note>in this period, "magnesia" usually referred to a variety of mineral ores, such as manganese or magnetite, rather than the modern element magnesium</note>, and marcasite <note>original: *markasita*; a term often used for iron pyrites or other metallic-looking sulfides</note>. It is mixed and joined with gold, nor is it ever [purified/separated] from it...

### On the Cleaning of Iron and Copper, Chapter XIII.

Take four heads of very good **garlic**, or as much as you wish, and grind them in a wooden **mortar** with a little **salt** and **alum** <term>alumen: a mineral salt used as a mordant and cleaning agent</term>. Then set it aside and pour over it the sharpest **vinegar**, until the "salt"—that is, the surface of the mixture—is covered. Let it be boiled well, just as we have already established.

### ->On Lead, Chapter XV.<-

The nature of **lead** <note>original: "usurub," a term derived from the Arabic *usrub*, meaning lead</note>. It is cold and dry, and it belongs to the domain of **Saturn** <note>In alchemy, lead is the metal associated with the planet Saturn due to its perceived "heaviness" and "slowness"</note>. In it are **gold** <term>aurum</term> and **silver** <term>argentum</term> essentially, though not visibly. It is a heavy body and slow of movement, and it is receptive to a **tincture** <note>A substance or "dye" used in alchemy to change the color and quality of a metal</note>; once it has received this tincture, it will never lose it. From lead comes silver, and lead is the cause of it. From it are made **litharge** <term>lithargyrum: lead monoxide, often used as a pigment or flux</term>, **ceruse** <term>cerussa: white lead, a common white pigment</term>, and **azrant** <note>Likely referring to minium or red lead</note>. From it also comes the greatest **Elixir** <term>elixir: the "philosopher's stone" or transformative agent</term> for the red and for the white <note>The "red" refers to the goal of making gold, and the "white" to the goal of making silver</note>. From lead, waters are also made, and by its means **quicksilver** <term>vivum argentum: mercury</term> is held <note>fixed or solidified</note>; its water is also suitable for iron, when...

...of multiplication. And **Pythagoras** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher, who was often cited by medieval alchemists as a foundational authority on the hidden nature of matter</note> said that **lead** <term>plumbum</term> is magnitude, and that everything is hidden within it, and that it is a "soft woman." He said that this woman has three qualities within herself: namely **blackness**, **whiteness**, and **redness** <note>original: *nigredo, albedo, rubedo*. These are the three standard stages of the alchemical "Great Work," representing decomposition, purification, and final perfection</note>. And it has four more qualities: namely moisture, liquefaction, speed, and dryness. This is because it is **sulfur-water** <term>aqua sulphurata</term>, and a burning coldness, because it extinguishes the heat of its masculine counterpart. For this reason, they gave it this name, "water of sulfur," and we have known nothing more capable, nor stronger, nor closer to it than that woman.

### On Alkali. Chapter XIX.

Take as much white **foliated alkali** <term>alcalt foliato</term> <note>original: *alcalt*. An alchemical term for alkaline salts, such as soda ash or potash, derived from plant ashes. ->On Alum: Chapter 20<-

### On the Same Subject, Chapter XXI

**The Nature of Alums.** Alum <term>alumen</term> <note>original: *alumen*. A class of chemical compounds, specifically hydrated double sulfates, highly valued in alchemy for their astringent and "cleansing" properties.</note> is a certain ointment which the dryness of the earth coagulates, and its mines are many. And the **son of Inthuel of Cordoba** <note>original: *filius Inthuelis Cordubensis*. This likely refers to an Arabic alchemical authority from Islamic Spain, possibly a Latinized name such as Ibn Thail.</note> said that alum has mines toward the South, in the territory of **Cordoba** in a certain place called **Agnamarasin**. 

And its nature is hot and dry, and it is a retainer of all **volatile substances** <term>volantiũ</term> <note>original: *volantium*, literally "flying things." In alchemy, this refers to spirits or elements like mercury and sulfur that evaporate or sublime when heated. ### Concerning Sal Ammoniac Chapter 24.

The nature of sal ammoniac. Know that sal ammoniac is much better and nobler than all salts in the preparation <note>original: *condimento*; literally "seasoning," but in this context refers to the alchemical treatment of substances</note>, because it dissolves **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</note>, and changes it into running water when it is exalted with it, and they placed it to dissolve in a humid place; and it is an ointment that the dryness of fire has coagulated. Its nature is hot and dry, and subtle, and it penetrates from one part to another; and it is a **flying spirit** <note>original: *spiritus volans*; a volatile substance that evaporates or sublimes when heated</note> and the helper of the **Alieśir** <term>Alieśir: an archaic spelling of *Elixir*, the substance believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold</term>. Were it not for this salt, the **Ylieśis** <term>Ylieśis: likely a variation of *Hyle*, referring to the primary matter of the world, or *Ilyasis*, a term for the perfected stone</term> would not be completed, nor would it be dissolved, nor would one thing be joined with another. In it, every hidden thing is concealed; it is that which enters and exits, and it is the **wax-maker** <term>incerator: from the process of "inceration," which involves adding liquid to a substance to make it soft like wax so it can be combined more easily</term> for all things.

<summary>This page defines Sal Ammoniac not just as a common chemical, but as a "spirit" essential for the creation of the Elixir, capable of softening substances and dissolving mercury.</summary>

The root of **sal ammoniac** <note>original: *salis armoniaci*; ammonium chloride, a volatile salt essential to medieval alchemy</note> comes from the dung and the fluids of animals, and it is especially formed in that place where the fire is made in a bathhouse, and at the bottom of the **cauldron** <note>original: *caldaria*</note>, and in those passages through which the fire is directed under the cauldrons. Afterward, its work is completed through **exaltation** <term>exaltatio: an alchemical process, usually sublimation, used to purify a substance and increase its perceived "potency" or spiritual dignity</term>. For this reason, it has made itself the greatest master over all salts, because in other salts such a **spirit** <note>original: *spiritus*; referring to the salt's ability to sublimate or "fly" from the fire</note> is not found as it is in this one; and it exists in the third transformation.

The renown of its operation is this: that it should be dissolved in a glazed jar with water or in a glass vessel; or place upon it three leaves of **smallage** <note>original: *apij*; wild celery, often used in organic alchemical additives</note> or **beet** <note>original: *betæ*</note>. It is better for it to be **exalted** <note>original: *exaltetur*; purified by sublimation</note> for you if you wish to work with it on two metals: **lead** or **copper** <note>original: *ære*; can refer to bronze, brass, or copper</note>, or **iron**. 

But if you work with it on **gold** or **silver**, you must exalt it seven times, and then it will be very good. And the renown of its operation is that you should take some part of sal ammoniac and grind it with an equal quantity of salt, and exalt—

<page-num>172</page-num>

<header>OF JOHN GARLAND</header>

### The Life of the Illustrious and Most
### Excellent Philosopher.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring two human figures (putti or classical figures) flanking the letter within a square frame.</image-desc>

**John Garland**, Grammarian and Poet, from his tender years of childhood, consecrated himself entirely to the **liberal arts** <note>original: *bonis artibus*; literally "the good arts," referring to the humanities and sciences</note> and such as that age provided. As the course of years progressed, while **England** was being devastated by the incursions of the **Danes**, he left the schools <note>original: *gymnasijs*; referring to centers of higher learning or academies</note> of his own country and betook himself to studies across the sea. There, he drank in the **liberal sciences** <term>liberal sciences: the traditional medieval curriculum consisting of the Trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the Quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy)</term> in greater abundance and with a full mouth <note>original: *ore pleno*; an idiom meaning he consumed the knowledge eagerly or copiously</note>, as if from the very fountainhead. With this constant diligence and industry following an honorable education, which he bestowed upon these studies, he easily surpassed every single person who was learning alongside him. This labor was so far from being useless that from it he immediately acquired <note>original: *compa-* (catchword for *comparavit*)</note> for himself an exceptional reputation for glory—

<vocab>John Garland, Grammarian, Poet, England, Danes, liberal sciences</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a biographical sketch of John Garland, a noted medieval English scholar. It describes how he fled the Danish invasions of England to pursue higher education in mainland Europe, where his dedication allowed him to surpass all his peers in the liberal arts.</summary>

he had acquired. For certain noblemen, delighted by his skill and eloquence, retained him under honorable conditions so that he might cultivate the youth in the elements of the Latin language and poetry. From this, a great number of students soon flocked to him, whom he inspired toward honorable studies through public lectures. Finally, for the benefit of posterity, he produced many small works, of which **Boston of Bury** <term>Boston of Bury: John Boston (active c. 1410), an English Benedictine monk and bibliographer famous for his "Catalog of the Writers of the Church," one of the first comprehensive union catalogs of library holdings.</term> in his great **Catalog** lists nearly all the following titles:
